# Roadmap — Stages 0–15 (condensed)

From the spec (§103–§120) and the Core Protocol Specification Plan (§91). Legend: ✅ done · 🔶 partial · ⏳ pending.

| Stage | Deliverable | Status |
|-------|-------------|--------|
| 0 | Freeze specs | ✅ `docs/protocol-spec-v1.0.md` (exact contract + cryptographic spec), crypto / vault / threat / invariants |
| 1 | Cryptographic reference + test vectors | ✅ TypeScript + Python (independent), Poseidon2 pinned by nargo vectors, `circuits/vectors/primitives.json` |
| 2 | Circuits (2-in/3-out) | ✅ compiled (Poseidon2, 23,343 constraints); KAT 18/18; soundness 17/17; **PP-C01/C02/C03 + PP-P01/P02 found and fixed**; external audit ⏳ |
| 3 | Asset vaults (native/ERC20/factory) | ✅ tested incl. the full token zoo and CREATE2 salt format |
| 4 | Immutable core | ✅ 4-aligned groups, txDomain binding, fixed-size payloads, custom errors, no-privilege scan |
| 5 | Deposit tests (malicious tokens, donation, reentrancy) | ✅ |
| 6 | Private transfer | ✅ real proofs for transfer / withdraw / merge / relayer-fee verify on-chain; e2e withdraw + fee spend; wallet-side proving ✅ (web: snarkjs in a Web Worker; native: `privacy-core/privacy-prover`, its proofs verified on-chain in forge) |
| 7 | Withdrawal (stealth recipient, claimable, fee) | ✅ claimable-fallback; private fee note; **fresh recipient by default** (the wallet pre-fills its next fresh EVM address, reserved only on confirm; pinned by `apps/web/test/first-launch.test.tsx`); stealth meta-addresses for third parties (ERC-5564) ⏳ — a private transfer to their `pp1…` address is the private way to pay them |
| 8 | Issuer attack lab | ✅ three vaults, asset-local damage, no cross-vault deficit |
| 9 | Reorg lab | ✅ block-based roots (forge) + reorg-aware indexer at depths 1/3/12/64 (TS) |
| 10 | Privacy lab vs the Tornado baseline | ✅ `docs/privacy-lab.md`: §68 targets met (amount unique-link 0 %; joint set 10 → 349 / 1,073) |
| 11 | Adversary ladder A1–A12 (A10/A11 removed) | ✅ `docs/threat-model.md` + `adversarial/` + `NoPrivilege.t.sol` |
| 12 | Proof benchmark + proof-system decision | 🔶 Groth16 measured (~0.9 s snarkjs, 285k gas verify; native Rust: witness ≈ 0.34 s + proof ≈ 0.24 s on a 28-thread desktop, `cargo run --release -p privacy-prover --example prove-bench`); phone runs + Halo2/FRI comparison ⏳ |
| 13 | Testnet | ⏳ (`contracts/script/Deploy.s.sol` dry-runs locally) |
| 14 | Independent audits | ⏳ |
| 15 | Production asset policy (ETH first, then USDC/USDT) | ⏳ owner decision — the wallet (`VITE_ERC20_VAULTS`), the relayer (`FEE_VAULTS`) and the swap engine (`ERC20_VAULTS`) already take any ERC-20 with a canonical vault by configuration |

| The Rust core in the web wallet (plan §43 "one core", §69) | `packages/sdk/src/core.ts`: a sync is two batches — every tree layer in one call (`treeLayers`) and note discovery over every payload in one call (`discoverNotes`) — run in the core when its WASM package is served from the wallet's own origin (`/core/`: `privacy-core/pkg/web` through the Vite plugin in dev, `npm run core:assets` → `public/core` for a build), loaded lazily on the first sync (never at first launch, §39), and in the TypeScript reference otherwise; `syncTreeQuorum` compares the raw views of every endpoint (leaf count, root, event digest) and hashes once; plans carry the synced layers so witness paths are lookups (`TxSpec.tree`); the reference's `MerkleLayers` makes the pure-TypeScript sync ≈ 8× cheaper too (one hash per leaf instead of ~8 + 2 per proof); "Advanced" shows which core is running; `apps/web/test/core-wasm.test.ts` runs sync / discovery / planning through both paths against one mock core (same tree, notes, witness) | ✅ |

| UniFFI bindings for the native apps (plan §40, §69) | `privacy-core/privacy-ffi`: the core + the native prover behind one UniFFI surface (keys, notes, nullifiers, Poseidon2, address, envelope, tree + discovery, phrase / keystore / journal, chain + dApp addresses, `Prover`); `privacy-core/uniffi-bindgen` generates Swift, Kotlin and Python from the compiled library (`privacy-core/scripts/ffi-bindings.mjs`); **11 Python tests over the generated bindings** against the shared vectors, the web wallet's keystore / journal, and a proof that is byte for byte the forge-verified native fixture | ✅ bindings (Python verified, Swift / Kotlin generated); app integration ⏳ (toolchains) |
| Native Groth16 prover (plan §40 "native proving") | `privacy-core/privacy-prover`: reads the snarkjs `.zkey` and `.wtns` natively, proves with arkworks Groth16 + the snarkjs QAP reduction (rayon on native hosts, single-threaded and std-free for wasm32), exports snarkjs JSON and the `transact` calldata; **the witness computed natively** (the circuit's WASM under wasmi, no JIT: iOS-safe) so circuit inputs → witness → proof is all Rust; 12 tests on the dev key + four v1 fixtures (incl. a forged statement refused) (key == `verification_key.json`, **native witness == snarkjs `.wtns`**, the soundness manifest's 4 honest / 10 attack verdicts reproduced, native proofs verify and bind every public input, snarkjs proofs verify natively, calldata == snarkjs export, unsatisfying witness rejected); **native proofs verified by the on-chain verifier** (`contracts/test/verifier/NativeRustProof.t.sol`, 5); witness ≈ 340 ms + proof ≈ 235 ms here vs ≈ 1.1 s for snarkjs (`examples/prove-bench.rs`); fixtures: `privacy-core/scripts/gen-prover-fixtures.mjs` | ✅ native witness + prover; UniFFI + device benchmarks ⏳ |
| 20 independent audits | `docs/audit-package.md`: scope, pinning, invariants/test inventory, known gaps, auditor questions; engagement itself external | 🔶 package ready |

## V2 — whale execution layer (plan "Privacy Wallet & Private Settlement Platform V2" §1–§137)
Spec first: `docs/whale-execution-spec-v1.0.md` (the 25 sections of plan §137), then the stages in order; each stage tested, run through `scripts/verify-all.mjs` and committed. Nothing in `PrivacyCore`, the vaults, the circuits or the escrow bytecode changes. UniswapX (§29) is deferred (spec §11: an escrow EIP-1271 change).

## V5 — Privacy Wallet Web V1 (plan "Privacy Wallet Web V1 — Mobile-First Privacy + Swap + Private Transfer Platform" §1–§88; the owner's complete rewrite is V6)
The plan's rule (§87): replace the UI foundation with a design system and an application shell first, then rebuild every feature inside it; the engines underneath stay. The spec `docs/privacy-wallet-web-spec-v1.0.md` freezes the product model, the four-tab shell and its routes, every screen's words, the tokens and the component inventory, the fee model (the relayer's percentage term through the existing fee note, the executor tip on swaps), the consumer bands `large` ($20K) and `large-protected` ($50K) below the untouched V4 bands, the one operation state machine with the mapping from every existing vocabulary, the error and recovery catalog, the Privacy Center's facts, the Classic Privacy module boundary, the SDK facade, the IndexedDB container with unchanged at-rest formats, and the gates. The owner's decisions: rebuild inside `apps/web/` (the §81 monorepo layout later), the lean UI stack (Tailwind, Radix primitives, viem + EIP-6963 with a lazy WalletConnect connector, zod), the services unchanged but the relayer's fee terms, Classic Privacy as screens on a pool interface with the contracts and the legal review later.

## V6 — Privacy Wallet Web V1, the owner's complete document (plan "Privacy Wallet Web V1 — Core Product → UX → Application → Privacy → Swap → Security → Production" §1–§96)
The owner's rewrite of the V5 document, complete (§89–§96 new: the exact development order, the final architecture, the Golden UX rule, the Definition of Done). The spec `docs/privacy-wallet-web-spec-v2.0.md` freezes it. Its closing instruction drives the pass: the actual monorepo, the design system, the shell and the five core mobile screens made visually correct (the V5 browser run's Simple-mode screenshots showed raw 18-decimal amounts, technical paragraphs, two competing primary buttons, a two-step "Fund now / Confirm the swap", wrapping badges, the desktop navigation at the bottom), then the engines connected underneath. The owner's decisions (2026-10-13): the full monorepo layout now (`apps/{web,relayer,api}`, `packages/{protocol,protocol-types,config,contracts,ui,sdk,wallet-engine,privacy-engine,risk-engine,swap-engine,server-kit}`, `privacy-core/`); the privacy fee 1 % of the input on every operation with the relayer/network cost on top (Protect through a new `ProtectRouter`, Send in the fee note, Swap through the escrow-funding fee note, the executor tip cut to the execution's gas cost); `apps/api` on Express 5 + `node:sqlite` with server records for swaps only; swaps funded right after one confirmation and signed automatically while the approved minimum holds; same-chain swaps delivered into the protected balance through a new `ShieldReceiver`; a GitHub Actions workflow for the offline gates.

A relayer that kept the connection open without answering held the failover on its terms (plan §85 rule 5: never depend on one relayer), and held a spend for good, and with it every spend queued behind it since one private spend runs at a time. `packages/sdk/src/relayer.ts`: a read (terms, a job, a status) ends after 20 s, a submission or a hand-over after 90 s, as `RelayerTimeout`. It is not a refusal, so a spend treats it as a lost answer and asks the relayer by nullifier before sending again, and it classifies as `relayer_unreachable`; the relayer answers a submission once sent, not mined. The API's reads (`apps/api/src/reads.ts`) had none either: a relayer that never answered held every quote (its terms) and every reconcile, and a hung RPC endpoint kept the next one from being asked. Sampled on the public gateways the same evening, it moved twice more in six minutes, once for 13 seconds, during a churn (six active vaults, six retiring). THORNode processes an inbound into any of its vaults that is not inactive (THORNode's `x/thorchain/handler_observed_tx_helpers.go`: only an inactive vault refunds). The wallet required the call's vault to equal the suggestion, and the wallet and the API both failed whenever the gateways differed on it, as one a block behind does around a move. Now, in the wallet (`packages/sdk/src/providerCall.ts`, `packages/sdk/src/guestGuard.ts`) and the API (`packages/swap-engine/src/adapters/thorchain.ts`): the router must agree on every gateway that answers and stays pinned; the vault is the one they all suggest, or one every gateway that answers lists among THORChain's active vaults (`/thorchain/vaults/asgard`: at least two must answer, and one that does not list it fails closed, so a lying gateway can neither add a vault nor outvote an honest one); gateways that differ on the suggestion confirm none; halted on any of them is halted. An accepted call whose vault has retired since is prepared afresh and checked in full (`packages/sdk/src/swapExecute.ts`). Found on the way: gateways that do not answer are an outage (`ProviderCallUnverified`: nothing signed, the scheduler's backoff; a protected execution's step tried again at its next tick), not a call that does something else. The check's wrapper had turned any other error into a mismatch, so before, an outage stopped a funded swap as unsafe too. A halt still stops a call as unsafe. Two settlement stalls in an hour open a provider's breaker (V3 §10), which steers every user's quotes away from it for five minutes. `GET /v1/track/:provider` took the stall's timing from the caller (`since`, `expected`) and keyed it by the caller's transaction: two requests from anyone about made-up transactions (`since=1&expected=1`) opened the breaker, as often as anyone liked. Its `tx` and `ref` also went to the provider's URL unchecked, as did the guest's `txid`. Now (`apps/api/src/routes/swap.ts`, `apps/api/src/store.ts`, `apps/api/src/db.ts`): a stall counts only for an operation the read names (an opaque `op_` id only its wallet holds), of that provider, whose execution the relayer made as exactly that transaction (recorded by a reconcile, else read from the relayer now), timed by the API's own records. Those records are the hand-over (`finalized_at`) and the plan's seconds, which finalize now records (`executions.expected_seconds`, the schema's second migration; the schema test pins each migration, and a version-1 database migrates keeping its rows). The caller's timing is ignored. The stall is keyed by the operation and timed by the API's clock. A `tx` or `txid` is 64 hex digits and a `ref` bounded plain characters, else `400 invalid_request` before the provider is asked. The wallet (`packages/sdk/src/orderTracker.ts`, `packages/sdk/src/swapService.ts`) sends its timing no more: when its own clock suspects a stall, the read names the order's operation. Tests: the API 38 (stall 3: made-up transactions with a caller's timing open nothing; finalize records the plan's seconds, a stall only past three times them by the API's clock, polled again it counts once, another transaction or a false id counts nothing, two operations open the breaker; an execution not yet reconciled is read from the relayer; schema +1; validation +1, the tracking queries); order-tracker +1 (the operation named past the wallet's threshold, never its timing); all fail on the old code. The relayer answered every failed request that was not a caller's mistake with the error's whole message, and viem writes the RPC's whole URL into its errors, with the request's body: an Alchemy- or Infura-style URL carries its key in the path. `/info` reads the gas price through the relayer's RPC on every call, so an RPC outage or rate limit (one a busy caller can bring on) handed the key to every wallet asking for the relayer's terms. A body that was not JSON was the relayer's own error (500). The API answered its own errors with their message too. Its fee-history reader named each failing RPC by its whole URL, and that text reached the wallet as the 503 `fee_history_unavailable`; a provider's 502 carried the provider's words whole. Now `packages/server-kit/src/redact.ts` `redactUrls` keeps a URL's scheme and host and drops its credentials, path, query and fragment. The relayer (`apps/relayer/src/answers.ts`: `errorAnswer`, `readJson`) answers a caller's mistake with its code and redacted words, and anything else with a bare `500 { code: "internal" }`; a body that is not JSON is `400 bad_json`. The request handler logs nothing, as before. The API (`apps/api/src/app.ts`) answers its own error with nothing of itself and redacts the 502 and the tip's words. `packages/swap-engine/src/tips.ts` names a failing RPC by its host. The wallet's catalog gains `bad_json` and `internal` (en/ko), as its coverage test of the services' codes requires. | 17 | follow-up: one transaction of the relayer's account at a time, and a send's fate is the node's answer. Three senders share the relayer's account: the submissions, the swap executor and the shield executor. Each read its nonce from the node's pending count while another send could be between that read and its broadcast, and the two executors' ticks run on the same interval. Two sends at once read one nonce. The node refused the second, or, priced 10 % higher, let it replace the first in the mempool. The swap executor recorded any send error as a failed execution (`execute-failed`, the user asked to confirm again or return the funds) or a failed refund (`refund-failed`, final: the funds stayed in the escrow). It recorded an RPC failure while estimating the same way. A swap, refund or shield transaction that never got a receipt (dropped, replaced, lost with a node's mempool) kept its job waiting for good. A submission whose broadcast answer was lost was `send_failed` although the node held it. `apps/relayer/src/sender.ts`: one `Sender` per account, shared by `Relayer.submit` and both executors (`server.ts`). A send reads its nonce once the previous one was broadcast or refused. It is signed before it is broadcast, so a broadcast error is checked against the node: a transaction the node holds was sent, one it does not know was refused (in the node's own words), and one that cannot be read is watched. The executors (`swapExecutor.ts`, `shieldExecutor.ts`) fail a call only on a revert at estimation. Anything else keeps the job as it is with a note, retried next tick. A transaction the node no longer knows 10 minutes after it was sent (`DROP_AFTER_MS`, the submissions' rule) sends its job back to the state it was sent from (`sent` on the job; an unpaid one gives its place in the budget back), to be sent again with a nonce read anew. If the dropped one lands after all, the second call reverts: an escrow executes once and refunds what it holds. Tests: the sender 9 (straight through viem two sends at once collide, through the sender the node takes all; a failed send does not stop the next; a lost answer the node holds is sent; a refusal is not; an unreadable fate is watched; a nonce that could not be read sends nothing; the reason without a URL's path; only `sender.ts` broadcasts, and the server hands its sender to both executors), the swap executor +3 (a refused execution is retried, not `execute-failed`; a dropped execution goes back and is sent again; an unestimated refund is retried, not `refund-failed`), the shield executor +1 (a dropped unpaid shield goes back, its budget returned, and is shielded again), the submissions +2 (a lost answer the node holds is submitted; a refusal is `send_failed` in the node's words, its notes free). The new executor and submission tests fail on the old code. The local stack's quick pass 196/196 (`scripts/local-stack/results/report-2026-10-15-v6-one-sender.json`); verify-all `--browser` 44/44 (the relayer 82, the API 41, the engine 111, sdk 122, web 543 + leakage 9, the server kit 13, 189 screenshots) | ✅ 2026-10-15 |

A caller's own entries stay on the left and buy nothing, and a request with fewer entries keeps its socket's address. `proxyHopsFrom` refuses anything but an integer 0–10, so a bad setting stops the service from starting. The API (`app.ts`, `server.ts`) and the relayer (`edge.ts`, `server.ts`) read it, and their `.env.example`, `docs/deployment.md` and the READMEs say when to set it. Tests: the server kit +1 (one and two proxies, a forged entry, no header, too few entries, the setting's parse), the API +1 and the relayer's edge +1 (behind one trusted proxy: a bucket per caller, a forged entry shares the caller's). | 17 | follow-up: the relayer executes swaps for the deployment's factory only. The swap executor took the factory from each request: it predicted the escrow from it, read the escrow's balance and, once that read said funded, sent `deployAndExecute` and later `deployAndRefund` to it. The shield executor has checked its factory since Stage 12; the swap executor never did. Anyone could therefore name a contract of their own as the factory and have the relayer call it at the relayer's gas. With an ERC-20 "asset" of their own, whose `balanceOf` says funded, nothing else was needed: an ERC-20 order is executed without the cost check (its tip is in the token). Their contract could burn up to the estimated gas and pay no tip, or, for an ETH order, take the escrow's tip itself (it deploys the escrow and chooses who is paid). `apps/relayer/src/swapExecutor.ts` `checkOrder`: an order whose factory is not `SWAP_FACTORY` is refused (`unknown_swap_factory`), and an executor without one admits none (`swap_unavailable`; the server answers `503` before reading the body, and a malformed `SWAP_FACTORY` stops the start). Jobs already in the journal are kept, as the operator's own. The wallet's catalog gains both codes, with words in English and Korean. The local stack's relayer gets the deployment's factory. Tests: the swap executor +1 (another factory refused for an execution and a refund; its own admitted in any case; none without one). The executors' suites run on the vector's factory. The new test fails on the old code. Still open: an ERC-20 order on this factory is executed without valuing its tip, so a token of no value, or a tip below the gas, costs the relayer the execution's gas (gap 47). The local stack's quick pass 197/197 (`scripts/local-stack/results/report-2026-10-15-v6-swap-factory.json`); verify-all `--browser` 44/44 (the relayer 90, the API 44, the engine 113, the server kit 16, sdk 122, web 543 + leakage 9, 189 screenshots) | ✅ 2026-10-15 |

Every protect, every order's refund note and every same-chain receipt is made out to the privacy address's owner commitment, so anyone can link a private swap to the public account that protected the funds (audit c18/c29/c37). The fix gives each deposited note its own owner key, derived from the note's secret: `packages/protocol/src/keys.ts` `deriveOwnerKey(spendKey, noteSecret)` = keccak256(be32(spendKey) ‖ "/owner-key/" ‖ be32(noteSecret)) mod p (a PRF of the spend key; the view key cannot derive it; the 75-byte preimage is apart from every other derivation), with vectors `packages/protocol/test/vectors/owner-keys.json` (`npm run owner-key-vectors`; the generator refuses to write a different file) and the Python twin `derive_owner_key`. Keyed by the secret rather than the counter (the funds review of the design): an opened payload or a refund's counter gives the key, so finding and spending a deposited note never depends on a counter search. | 17 | follow-up (audit 2026-10-16, c40): an older tab never writes its old journal over a newer one. Each tab's secure store is a mirror of IndexedDB filled when the page opened (`apps/web/src/storage/secureStore.ts`); one unlocked tab at a time (2026-10-14) kept two tabs from writing at once, but a tab opened before the other tab's last changes unlocked to its own old copy — and its unlock journals at once — so the newer journal (a swap, a note, a fresh address handed out) was gone, and once the other tab stepped aside this one ran on the old copy. `SecureStore.refresh` reads IndexedDB again after its own writes land; `WalletSession.unlock` reads the store again first, and `WalletSession.reload` reads the journal again when the tab is granted the Web Lock (`apps/web/src/platform/activeTab.ts`, before anything of it runs: the other tab wrote until it stepped aside). The session journals the note store only when its notes changed (an unlock's load no longer rewrites the journal it just read), and a new wallet stores its journal at once. The local stack's run caught a race in the first version: a tab granted the lock that journaled again while it read its journal asked for the lock once more and queued behind itself ("The wallet is open in another tab" with one tab open); a tab taking the wallet over now asks for nothing. Docs: the wallet spec §8, security invariants. Tests: `storage` (a second store sees another's writes and removals after refresh), `active-tab` (tabs with their own copies: an older tab unlocks to the newer journal and writes nothing older back; a waiting tab reads what the other wrote as it stepped aside; a tab that journals while it takes the wallet over stays active, with a slow re-read and a lock manager that answers late) — failing on the old code. | 17 | follow-up (audit 2026-10-16, c10/c13): a smaller token delivery reaches the protected balance, and one revert no longer ends a shield job. A same-chain receipt pays its tip only on a delivery of at least its minimum (a hundred tips). Below it — a lower minimum the user accepted at signing, a partly filled streamed THORChain swap — the relayer's shield is unpaid, and the executor's floor for an unpaid token shield was that same minimum: the delivery stayed at the receiver, whose payload only the wallet and the relayer know, while the order read "adding it to your protected balance". Its gas was estimated on every tick, and the job never ended. `apps/relayer/src/shieldExecutor.ts`: a token's unpaid floor is twice the receipt's tip (the shield's gas at quote time, in the token; ETH keeps twice the gas), checked before any estimate; a job whose delivery is still below its floor when the watch ends ends (`expired`, the balance noted) — 1-wei dust no longer holds a job open for good (part of c11). The spec's sentence that any shieldable delivery pays the tip, a lower accepted minimum included, is corrected. And one reverted estimate ended a job for good (`shield-failed`), as did one mined shield that reverted; the wallet, finding the job, never registered it again, so a delivery the job had seen stayed at the receiver (c13). A shield that would revert (a paused token, a race) is now estimated again every 10 minutes (`ESTIMATE_RETRY_SECONDS`) and fails only when the watch ends; a mined one that reverted sends the job back to read the receiver again (somebody may have shielded first), at most twice (`MAX_REVERTED_SHIELDS`). Docs: the wallet spec §11.2, the relayer README. Tests: `shieldExecutor` +3, one rewritten (30 tips shielded once the swap is over; one tip never estimated, the job ended with the watch; a reverting estimate retried and failed only at the watch's end; two mined reverts sent back, the third fails) — failing on the old code. | 17 | follow-up (2026-10-23, audit round 2, n16): a protected execution keeps an answer's alert when its alerts are full. The merge of an answer given mid-tick took what it added by length; once a campaign held its 200 alerts the cap dropped the oldest and the answer's own alert ("Paused by you") was lost, and the entries the tick had journaled before the answer came back twice. `packages/sdk/src/wallet/campaigns.ts` merges by identity, then caps (`packages/sdk/src/wallet/alerts.ts` exports the cap). Tests: `campaign-runner` +1 — failing on the old code. The local stack's quick pass 198/198; verify-all `--browser` 44/44 | ✅ 2026-10-23 |
| 17 | follow-up (2026-10-23, audit round 2, n05 with n09): the engine's choice stays on an order through every rewrite. Only an order whose provider the engine chose at the commit counts a guard refusal against that provider; a prepare's own write of the order, a restore's and a resume's dropped the mark, and any later refusal never counted. `apps/api/src/routes/swap.ts` keeps it. With n09: `packages/swap-engine/.env.example` no longer says ERC20_VAULTS is written as the wallet's VITE_ERC20_VAULTS (whose symbol comes first; a copied value stops the start). Tests: `divergence` +1 — failing on the old code. The local stack's quick pass 198/198; verify-all `--browser` 44/44 | ✅ 2026-10-23 |
| 17 | follow-up (2026-10-23, audit round 2, n13 with n26): the tree's view is read at one block. Once an endpoint refused the whole history for its size, the note events were read in windows up to a block taken after the root's read, and an insert in between failed the sync as a stale view; every sync also asked that endpoint the whole history first again. `packages/sdk/src/chain.ts` reads the leaf count and the root at one block and the events up to it, and an endpoint that refused once is read in windows at once (remembered by URL). A refused window stays halved for the rest of its read (grown back, a fixed cap would refuse it again). With n26: the Privacy Center no longer says the pool reads are the same for every user — the wallet reads its own notes' spent state, receipts and escrows too, and it says so. Tests: `log-windows` +3, `privacyCenter` +2 assertions — failing on the old code. The local stack's quick pass 198/198; verify-all `--browser` 44/44 | ✅ 2026-10-23 |

The risk database dates its incidents by day, and read as that day's midnight such an incident opened its provider's breaker only until the next midnight (never when recorded the day after), and a protected execution read one dated the day it started as before it. `packages/protocol/src/wallet/riskClasses.ts` `incidentSpan` (a date by day: the whole UTC day); the breaker keeps it fresh until a day after its end (`packages/swap-engine/src/health.ts`, `engine.ts`), the runner compares its day's end with the operation's start. The R4 rule's age and the score's decay are as before. Tests: `riskClasses` +1, `risk` +1, `engine-v2` +1 — the engine's failing on the old code. ## V7 — Privacy Wallet Web V1.1 (plan "Privacy Wallet Web V1.1 — Master Development Plan" §1–§15)
The owner's master plan of 2026-10-26: two products inside one website — the Privacy Wallet (the V6 product) and **Tornado Classic**, an original-style interface for the existing Ethereum mainnet pools 0.1 / 1 / 10 / 100 ETH with the original note format, built as a separately locked, separately licensed `classic/` app — plus the information architecture (`/classic`, `/privacy`, `/settings`, More = Tornado Classic / Privacy Center / Settings, Connect wallet in the header), the fee policy statement, the amount policy, the swap provider facade and the document's fifteen operation states. The spec `docs/privacy-wallet-web-spec-v3.0.md` freezes it (v2.0 stays normative where v3.0 says nothing). The owner's decisions (2026-10-26): keep 1 % per operation and disclose it (a Protect then a Swap pay twice; the privacy fee is earned at funding; a published fee & refund policy); $50K–$100K = one order by default, split only when the plan says staged or the user asks, never identical pieces; over $100K behind the Advanced experience with a liquidity qualification; the operation states renamed to the document's fifteen while the lean stack and the package names stay; Classic keeps the original wording under its own word policy; live Classic transactions only after the legal review is recorded and the per-pool gates pass, the original's sanctions-oracle check and geofence behind switches, off; MIT for our code, GPL-3.0 for the Classic bundle. Verified on chain while planning (2026-10-26): every pool's `operator()` is renounced and `ROOT_HISTORY_SIZE()` is 100; the router's instance registry lists all four pools enabled.

## Decisions still open (owner needed)
- **Gas vs §79 targets** — `transact` 1.79M / `deposit` 1.52M vs 650k / 150k. At today's gas that is
  under a dollar per operation; the levers (direct library calls, Yul body hash, deposit queue,
  shallower tree, proven updates) are in `benchmarks/gas/README.md`.
- **Proof system** (plan §41–42): Groth16 + ceremony, or a universal/transparent system — needs the
  Halo2/FRI benchmark.
- **Asset policy (plan §36, stage 15)** — the wallet, the relayer and the swap service now handle any ERC-20 with a canonical vault (config-driven: `VITE_ERC20_VAULTS`, `FEE_VAULTS`, `ERC20_VAULTS`); WHICH tokens get vaults on the production deployment remains the owner's call.
- **Payload encryption scheme** — *proposal implemented* (`crypto-spec.md`, "Output payload envelope"): view key → X25519 via a tagged sha256, envelope key from the shared secret + both public keys, bech32m `pp1…` privacy address; reference tests + vectors, wallet note discovery on it. The owner confirms or picks another binding under a new version byte.

## Private submission (plan §76, §81, §85 rule 5) — built
`apps/relayer` + the wallet's submission setting: by default a relayer submits every private
transaction from its own key and is paid by a private fee note (output 1 of a withdrawal, output 2 of a
transfer, encrypted to the relayer's privacy address); the connected wallet remains the public fallback and
any relayer URL can be used. The same service runs the **swap-order executor**: the Swap tab hands it the
funded order's params, the signed execution and the refund payload; it deploys + executes for the tip and
refunds to the pre-committed note after the window (12 executor tests vs the periphery forge vector). The
Swap tab funds the escrow first and signs the provider call against a fresh quote afterwards (quotes are
minutes, the delay budget hours), re-signing if the executor reports expiry. Journals are file-backed and
survive restarts; per-caller rate limits and a job cap protect a public relayer. The wallet merges notes
automatically (private self-transfers, fee-aware) when a spend needs more than two. `scripts/verify-all.mjs`
runs every suite + builds (+ live, + forge) in one command. Not run on a live chain (no node on this machine).

## Deferred (and why)
- zkAPI-metered RPC / quote gateways (plan §52–§53, §64–§65): the live zkAPI deployments issue OpenRouter keys only; a fork with another capability issuer cannot run on this machine (Winsock LSP). Tor / mixnet transport (§7): native-host work (`docs/mobile.md`). RN / Tauri shells (§40, §42, §69–§70): no Xcode / Android SDK / MSVC here; the shared core and engine are built and verified. The private API marketplace (§54): "eventually" in the plan.
- Testnet adapter pins (provider testnets) and the Sepolia broadcast; (a live provider-volume feed is not needed: cover traffic counts only for a provider that keeps the escrow → destination link private, and none does — `docs/privacy-lab.md`); proving benchmarks on phones (the native prover and its benchmark are built: `docs/mobile.md`); Halo2/FRI comparison; production
ceremony + verifier; external audits; everything in plan §92 (UI beyond the shell, relayer, website).
